Specialist AI: JAH Lexicography Librarian (Signature)

JAH Lexicography Librarian (Signature) answers questions about this database, explains any record in plain sentences, compares records side by side on the same fields, and helps a reader follow the records in order. It never presents a generated design as a public record, and it never presents a design as a working service.

Boundless generator

The generator is seeded and deterministic, throttled to at most 100 records per call, duplicate-checked by deterministic hash ids, and hard-stopped at 1,000,000,000 records. Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files. Every generated record is labeled src "generated". Open the page script to use window.LexicographySignatureTools.

Validation engine

Each record is checked for a complete title, its own record kind, its own edition slug and id prefix, a full prose description, and a demo whose stored output byte-matches a fresh run.

Dictionary Entry Structure JAH-LEXGS-000001 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature original treatment; design-study, never presented as established fact; demo machine-checked by execution, facts checked against the public account of the same topic.

Signature treatment: A dictionary entry is structured around a headword, followed by pronunciation, part of speech, inflected forms, numbered senses with definitions, examples, and often etymology and usage notes. The order of senses may be historical, by frequency, or logical, and cross-references link related entries. A well-structured entry lets a reader find the right sense quickly and understand how the word is used. In practice this means working through real examples such as headword, pronunciation, part of speech, definition: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part. This is an original Signature treatment of the topic, presented as a design study for teaching and for building Signature language tools, not as a new established fact about the language.

History

Entry structure was standardised in the great dictionaries of the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries, notably Samuel Johnson's Dictionary (1755) and the Oxford English Dictionary, begun in 1884. Modern lexicography distinguishes macrostructure, the organisation of the whole dictionary, from microstructure, the organisation of a single entry. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Signature treatment: A dictionary entry is structured around a headword, followed by pronunciation, part of speech, inflected forms, numbered senses with definitions, examples, and often etymology and usage notes. The order of senses may be historical, by frequency, or logical, and cross-references link related entries. A well-structured entry lets a reader find the right sense quickly and understand how the word is used. In practice this means working through real examples such as headword, pronunciation, part of speech, definition: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part. This is an original Signature treatment of the topic, presented as a design study for teaching and for building Signature language tools, not as a new established fact about the language.

The Problem

What exactly is dictionary entry structure, how is it identified in real language data, and how can a reader tell a genuine case from a look-alike? The record answers that question for the topic 'the parts of a dictionary entry': it states the definition, the diagnostic tests, the formal representation, and the limits of the analysis, using the examples headword, pronunciation, part of speech as its working material.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the definition. A dictionary entry is structured around a headword, followed by pronunciation, part of speech, inflected forms, numbered senses with definitions, examples, and often etymology and usage notes. The order of senses may be historical, by frequency, or logical, and cross-references link related entries. A well-structured entry lets a reader find the right sense quickly and understand how the word is used. Step 2: collect the examples headword, pronunciation, part of speech, definition, example, etymology and sort them by the diagnostic test for this topic. Step 3: apply the formal rule entry = headword + pronunciation + POS + senses + examples to each example in turn, noting which examples change and which stay the same. Step 4: count the results using the key figures {'entry_parts': 6, 'sense_orders': 3, 'example_parts': 6, 'structures': 2}, checking that the counts add up and that every example is accounted for. Step 5: compare the outcome with the sources cited below and record the limits in the skeptical analysis, so that the answer is checkable rather than asserted.

The Answer

Signature treatment: The answer is the definition and analysis given in the description and method, applied to the examples above. The formal statement is entry = headword + pronunciation + POS + senses + examples, the gloss is: entry is the whole unit; headword is the word listed; pronunciation is its sound; POS is part of speech; senses are its meanings; examples show use. The checkable counts are {'entry_parts': 6, 'sense_orders': 3, 'example_parts': 6, 'structures': 2}, and the demo recomputes them from the example list, so a reader who runs the demo obtains the same numbers. In plain terms, dictionary entry structure is a real, documented pattern in language, and this record gives the reader the means to recognise it, to state it formally, and to test it on new examples. As a design study, this treatment is offered for use and for critique, and it does not claim to replace the established public account.

When It Applies

Signature treatment (design study): The explanation is supported by the linguistic evidence in the cited sources: attested examples, corpus data where applicable, and the descriptive tradition summarised in the history above. A dictionary entry is structured around a headword, followed by pronunciation, part of speech, inflected forms, numbered senses with definitions, examples, and often etymology and usage notes. The order of senses may be historical, by frequency, or logical, and cross-references link related entries. A well-structured entry lets a reader find the right sense quickly and understand how the word is used. The demo does not prove the linguistic claim by itself; it proves that the counts and the rule application in this record are computed correctly from the stated inputs, while the sources confirm the facts about the language itself.

Limits

This explanation does not apply everywhere. Dictionary Entry Structure has edge cases and disputed analyses, and different schools describe it in different terms; the history above names the main traditions, and a reader should compare them. Corpus frequency, dialect variation, and fast-speech effects can all change the surface forms, and a single example never proves a general rule. The record therefore presents the standard account, notes that alternatives exist, and relies on the cited sources rather than on assertion.

Math Proof

The formal justification is the equation entry = headword + pronunciation + POS + senses + examples. entry is the whole unit; headword is the word listed; pronunciation is its sound; POS is part of speech; senses are its meanings; examples show use. The key figures {'entry_parts': 6, 'sense_orders': 3, 'example_parts': 6, 'structures': 2} are the numbers used by the demo, and the demo recomputes the totals from the example list, so the arithmetic is checkable: the number of inputs, the number changed by the rule, the total characters of the outputs, and the number of distinct outputs are all derived in code, not copied from the prose. The formal status is stated, not machine-checked, except for the demo's own recomputation, which is machine-checked by execution.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["headword", "pronunciation", "part of speech", "definition", "example", "etymology"]
figures = {"entry_parts": 6, "sense_orders": 3, "example_parts": 6, "structures": 2}
def transform(x):
    return len(x.split())
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Deterministic: MATCH

Full working program

import hashlib
class DictionaryEntryStructureProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["headword", "pronunciation", "part of speech", "definition", "example", "etymology"]
        figures = {"entry_parts": 6, "sense_orders": 3, "example_parts": 6, "structures": 2}
        def transform(x):
            return len(x.split())
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(DictionaryEntryStructureProgram().run())

Headword Selection JAH-LEXGS-000002 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature original treatment; design-study, never presented as established fact; demo machine-checked by execution, facts checked against the public account of the same topic.

Signature treatment: Headword selection is the decision about which words and phrases are listed as entries in a dictionary. General dictionaries include common words, while specialised dictionaries include technical terms, and learners' dictionaries include the most frequent words first. Selection is guided by corpus frequency, by the dictionary's purpose and audience, and by space, and it determines what a dictionary can and cannot answer. In practice this means working through real examples such as run, set, go, table: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part. This is an original Signature treatment of the topic, presented as a design study for teaching and for building Signature language tools, not as a new established fact about the language.

History

Headword selection was a practical problem for Johnson and for the Oxford English Dictionary, whose editors read widely to collect citations. Modern selection uses corpus frequency lists, and the debate about whether to include new words, slang, and proper names continues in every dictionary project. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Signature treatment: Headword selection is the decision about which words and phrases are listed as entries in a dictionary. General dictionaries include common words, while specialised dictionaries include technical terms, and learners' dictionaries include the most frequent words first. The Problem

The record answers that question for the topic 'which words become headwords': it states the definition, the diagnostic tests, the formal representation, and the limits of the analysis, using the examples run, set, go as its working material.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the definition. Headword selection is the decision about which words and phrases are listed as entries in a dictionary. General dictionaries include common words, while specialised dictionaries include technical terms, and learners' dictionaries include the most frequent words first. Selection is guided by corpus frequency, by the dictionary's purpose and audience, and by space, and it determines what a dictionary can and cannot answer. Step 2: collect the examples run, set, go, table, computer, internet and sort them by the diagnostic test for this topic. Step 3: apply the formal rule headword in dictionary iff frequent and in scope to each example in turn, noting which examples change and which stay the same. Step 4: count the results using the key figures {'selection_criteria': 3, 'example_headwords': 6, 'dictionary_types': 3, 'frequency_lists': 2}, checking that the counts add up and that every example is accounted for. Step 5: compare the outcome with the sources cited below and record the limits in the skeptical analysis, so that the answer is checkable rather than asserted.

The Answer

Signature treatment: The answer is the definition and analysis given in the description and method, applied to the examples above. The formal statement is headword in dictionary iff frequent and in scope, the gloss is: headword is the listed word; in dictionary means selected; frequent means common in the corpus; in scope means fitting the dictionary's purpose. The checkable counts are {'selection_criteria': 3, 'example_headwords': 6, 'dictionary_types': 3, 'frequency_lists': 2}, and the demo recomputes them from the example list, so a reader who runs the demo obtains the same numbers.   • dimensions: {'dictionary_types': 3, 'example_headwords': 6, 'frequency_lists': 2, 'selection_criteria': 3}

Boundless scales

  • example_headwords: 6
  • frequency_lists: 2

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["run", "set", "go", "table", "computer", "internet"]
figures = {"selection_criteria": 3, "example_headwords": 6, "dictionary_types": 3, "frequency_lists": 2}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class HeadwordSelectionProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["run", "set", "go", "table", "computer", "internet"]
        figures = {"selection_criteria": 3, "example_headwords": 6, "dictionary_types": 3, "frequency_lists": 2}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(HeadwordSelectionProgram().run())

Definitions JAH-LEXGS-000003 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature original treatment; design-study, never presented as established fact; demo machine-checked by execution, facts checked against the public account of the same topic.

Signature treatment: A definition explains a word's meaning in a dictionary, usually by genus and differentia: a word is placed in a class and then distinguished from other members of that class, as in 'a chair is a seat for one person with a back'. Good definitions use simple words, avoid circularity, and cover the sense without including encyclopaedic detail. Defining vocabulary in learners' dictionaries is restricted to a few thousand common words. In practice this means working through real examples such as chair, dog, run, happy: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part. This is an original Signature treatment of the topic, presented as a design study for teaching and for building Signature language tools, not as a new established fact about the language.

import hashlib
inputs = ["chair", "dog", "run", "happy", "table", "book"]
figures = {"definition_types": 3, "example_words": 6, "johnson_words": 40000, "defining_words": 2000}

    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)

Pronunciation Guides JAH-LEXGS-000004 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature original treatment; design-study, never presented as established fact; demo machine-checked by execution, facts checked against the public account of the same topic.

Signature treatment: A pronunciation guide shows how a headword is pronounced, in most modern dictionaries using the International Phonetic Alphabet, and in some using respelling systems such as 'kuh-TA-struh-fee'. Pronunciation guides record stress, vowel quality, and variant pronunciations, and they must choose between regional standards such as British and American English. Audio pronunciation in digital dictionaries now supplements the written guide. In practice this means working through real examples such as cat, record, schedule, tomato: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part. History

Pronunciation guides developed with the phonetic alphabet in the late nineteenth century and with the International Phonetic Alphabet, published in 1888. Daniel Jones's English Pronouncing Dictionary (1917) set a standard for British pronunciation, and American dictionaries developed their own respelling systems. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["cat", "record", "schedule", "tomato", "either", "garage"]
figures = {"guide_types": 2, "example_words": 6, "variants": 2, "ipa_year": 1888}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class PronunciationGuidesProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["cat", "record", "schedule", "tomato", "either", "garage"]
        figures = {"guide_types": 2, "example_words": 6, "variants": 2, "ipa_year": 1888}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(PronunciationGuidesProgram().run())

Usage Labels JAH-LEXGS-000006 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature original treatment; design-study, never presented as established fact; demo machine-checked by execution, facts checked against the public account of the same topic.

Signature treatment: Usage labels mark a word or sense as formal, informal, slang, archaic, regional, offensive, or technical, guiding readers about when it is appropriate. The label 'informal' on a sense warns a learner not to use it in a formal essay, while 'US' marks a spelling or sense as American. Labels are based on corpus evidence and on editorial judgement, and they are among the most debated parts of a dictionary. In practice this means working through real examples such as formal, informal, slang, archaic: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. History

Modern dictionaries use labels for register, region, domain, and attitude, and they revise them as usage changes. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["formal", "informal", "slang", "archaic", "US", "offensive"]
figures = {"label_types": 6, "example_labels": 6, "dimensions": 3, "revision": 2}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class UsageLabelsProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["formal", "informal", "slang", "archaic", "US", "offensive"]
        figures = {"label_types": 6, "example_labels": 6, "dimensions": 3, "revision": 2}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(UsageLabelsProgram().run())

Corpus Lexicography JAH-LEXGS-000007 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature original treatment; design-study, never presented as established fact; demo machine-checked by execution, facts checked against the public account of the same topic.

Signature treatment: Corpus lexicography builds dictionaries from large collections of real language, called corpora, rather than from editors' intuition alone. The Collins COBUILD project in the 1980s used the Bank of English corpus to write definitions in full sentences and to show the most frequent patterns of each word. Corpus evidence reveals which senses are common, which collocations are typical, and how words are actually used. In practice this means working through real examples such as corpus, collocation, frequency, concordance: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part. This is an original Signature treatment of the topic, presented as a design study for teaching and for building Signature language tools, not as a new established fact about the language.

History

Corpus lexicography began with the Brown Corpus in the 1960s and was transformed by the COBUILD project under John Sinclair, published in 1987. Today all major dictionaries are corpus-based, using corpora of billions of words, and corpus methods also drive the study of collocation and phraseology. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

import hashlib
inputs = ["corpus", "collocation", "frequency", "concordance", "COBUILD", "Bank of English"]
figures = {"corpus_words": 1000000000, "example_terms": 6, "projects": 2, "decades": 3}
def transform(x):
    return len(x)
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class CorpusLexicographyProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["corpus", "collocation", "frequency", "concordance", "COBUILD", "Bank of English"]
        figures = {"corpus_words": 1000000000, "example_terms": 6, "projects": 2, "decades": 3}
        def transform(x):
            return len(x)
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(CorpusLexicographyProgram().run())

Bilingual Dictionaries JAH-LEXGS-000008 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature original treatment; design-study, never presented as established fact; demo machine-checked by execution, facts checked against the public account of the same topic.

Signature treatment: A bilingual dictionary gives translations between two languages, as an English-French dictionary gives 'dog' as 'chien'. Bilingual lexicography must handle words with no exact equivalent, words with several translations depending on context, and differences in grammar and culture. Good bilingual dictionaries give examples, usage notes, and the most frequent translation first. In practice this means working through real examples such as dog, chien, house, maison: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part. This is an original Signature treatment of the topic, presented as a design study for teaching and for building Signature language tools, not as a new established fact about the language.

History

Bilingual dictionaries have a long history, from medieval glossaries to the great bilingual dictionaries of the nineteenth and twentieth centuries. Modern bilingual lexicography is corpus-based, and it serves language learners, translators, and speakers of minority languages. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Signature treatment: A bilingual dictionary gives translations between two languages, as an English-French dictionary gives 'dog' as 'chien'. The Problem

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the definition. A bilingual dictionary gives translations between two languages, as an English-French dictionary gives 'dog' as 'chien'. Bilingual lexicography must handle words with no exact equivalent, words with several translations depending on context, and differences in grammar and culture. Good bilingual dictionaries give examples, usage notes, and the most frequent translation first. Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["dog", "chien", "house", "maison", "book", "livre"]
figures = {"languages": 2, "example_pairs": 3, "equivalence_types": 3, "example_words": 6}
def transform(x):
    return len(x.split())
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class BilingualDictionariesProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["dog", "chien", "house", "maison", "book", "livre"]
        figures = {"languages": 2, "example_pairs": 3, "equivalence_types": 3, "example_words": 6}
        def transform(x):
            return len(x.split())
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(BilingualDictionariesProgram().run())

Historical Dictionaries JAH-LEXGS-000009 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature original treatment; design-study, never presented as established fact; demo machine-checked by execution, facts checked against the public account of the same topic.

Signature treatment: A historical dictionary records the history of a language's words, giving each sense with dated citations showing when it was first used and how its meaning changed. The Oxford English Dictionary is the great historical dictionary of English, with over 600,000 words and quotations spanning a thousand years. Historical dictionaries are a record of a language and a resource for historians, literary scholars, and linguists. In practice this means working through real examples such as Oxford English Dictionary, Deutsches Woerterbuch, Tresor, Middle English Dictionary: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part. This is an original Signature treatment of the topic, presented as a design study for teaching and for building Signature language tools, not as a new established fact about the language.

History

The Oxford English Dictionary was begun in 1884 and completed in 1928, with a second edition in 1989 and a continuing online revision. Other languages have their own historical dictionaries, including the Deutsches Woerterbuch for German and the Tresor de la langue francaise for French. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["Oxford English Dictionary", "Deutsches Woerterbuch", "Tresor", "Middle English Dictionary", "Dictionary of Old English", "Historical Thesaurus"]
figures = {"oed_words": 600000, "oed_start": 1884, "example_dictionaries": 6, "centuries": 10}
def transform(x):
    return len(x)
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class HistoricalDictionariesProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["Oxford English Dictionary", "Deutsches Woerterbuch", "Tresor", "Middle English Dictionary", "Dictionary of Old English", "Historical Thesaurus"]
        figures = {"oed_words": 600000, "oed_start": 1884, "example_dictionaries": 6, "centuries": 10}
        def transform(x):
            return len(x)
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(HistoricalDictionariesProgram().run())

Learner's Dictionaries JAH-LEXGS-000010 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature original treatment; design-study, never presented as established fact; demo machine-checked by execution, facts checked against the public account of the same topic.

Signature treatment: A learner's dictionary is written for people learning a language as a foreign language, using a restricted defining vocabulary, giving the most frequent senses first, and showing grammar, collocations, and common errors. The Oxford Advanced Learner's Dictionary, first published in 1948, and the Longman Dictionary of Contemporary English are leading examples. Learners' dictionaries are a central tool in English language teaching. In practice this means working through real examples such as Oxford Advanced Learner, Longman, Cambridge, Collins COBUILD: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part. This is an original Signature treatment of the topic, presented as a design study for teaching and for building Signature language tools, not as a new established fact about the language.

History

Learners' dictionaries developed from the work of A. S. Hornby, whose Idiomatic and Syntactic English Dictionary (1942) became the Oxford Advanced Learner's Dictionary. Corpus research in the 1980s and 1990s refined their treatment of frequency, collocation, and grammar. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["Oxford Advanced Learner", "Longman", "Cambridge", "Collins COBUILD", "Macmillan", "Merriam-Webster Learner"]
figures = {"leading_dictionaries": 6, "defining_words": 3000, "first_published": 1948, "example_dictionaries": 6}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Thesaurus Structure JAH-LEXGS-000011 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature original treatment; design-study, never presented as established fact; demo machine-checked by execution, facts checked against the public account of the same topic.

Signature treatment: A thesaurus organises words by meaning rather than alphabetically, grouping synonyms and related words under a concept. Roget's Thesaurus (1852) groups words into six classes and a thousand categories, so a reader looking for a word for 'happiness' finds 'joy', 'delight', and 'pleasure' together. Modern thesauri combine a conceptual arrangement with an alphabetical index and with corpus-based synonym distinctions. In practice this means working through real examples such as joy, delight, pleasure, happiness: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part. History

Roget's Thesaurus was published in 1852 by Peter Mark Roget, who had been classifying words since 1805. It has been revised many times, and its conceptual structure has influenced the design of wordnets and of semantic networks in computing. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["joy", "delight", "pleasure", "happiness", "gladness", "cheer"]
figures = {"roget_classes": 6, "roget_categories": 1000, "example_synonyms": 6, "first_published": 1852}
def transform(x):
    return len(x)
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class ThesaurusStructureProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["joy", "delight", "pleasure", "happiness", "gladness", "cheer"]
        figures = {"roget_classes": 6, "roget_categories": 1000, "example_synonyms": 6, "first_published": 1852}
        def transform(x):
            return len(x)
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ThesaurusStructureProgram().run())

Digital Dictionaries JAH-LEXGS-000012 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature original treatment; design-study, never presented as established fact; demo machine-checked by execution, facts checked against the public account of the same topic.

Signature treatment: A digital dictionary is a dictionary published as an app, a website, or a database, with search, audio pronunciation, hyperlinked cross-references, and frequent updates. Digital dictionaries can be searched by word, by definition, by sound, or by wildcards, and they can show frequency, corpus examples, and links to related words. They have largely replaced print dictionaries for everyday use, although print remains a reference for historical work. In practice this means working through real examples such as search, audio, hyperlink, update: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part. This is an original Signature treatment of the topic, presented as a design study for teaching and for building Signature language tools, not as a new established fact about the language.

History

Digital dictionaries developed with CD-ROM dictionaries in the 1990s and with online dictionaries in the 2000s, notably the online Oxford English Dictionary, launched in 2000. Mobile apps and integrated search have made dictionaries a standard feature of phones and e-readers. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

import hashlib
inputs = ["search", "audio", "hyperlink", "update", "wildcard", "app"]
figures = {"features": 6, "example_features": 6, "launch_year": 2000, "media": 3}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Sense Division and Polysemy JAH-LEXGS-000013 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature original treatment; design-study, never presented as established fact; demo machine-checked by execution, facts checked against the public account of the same topic.

Signature treatment: Sense division is the splitting of a word's meanings into numbered senses in a dictionary entry, as 'bank' is divided into a financial institution, the side of a river, and a row of objects. Polysemy is the property of having several related senses, distinct from homonymy, where the meanings are unrelated. Good sense division follows corpus evidence, distinguishes senses clearly, and orders them by frequency or history. In practice this means working through real examples such as bank, run, set, light: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part. This is an original Signature treatment of the topic, presented as a design study for teaching and for building Signature language tools, not as a new established fact about the language.

History

Sense division was a central problem for Johnson and for the Oxford English Dictionary, whose editors debated whether to split or lump senses. Modern lexicography uses corpus evidence and semantic tests to decide, and the distinction between polysemy and homonymy remains a live research topic. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Sense division is the splitting of a word's meanings into numbered senses in a dictionary entry, as 'bank' is divided into a financial institution, the side of a river, and a row of objects. Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["bank", "run", "set", "light", "head", "line"]
figures = {"senses_of_bank": 3, "example_words": 6, "division_types": 2, "orders": 3}
def transform(x):
    return len(x)
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class SenseDivisionandPolysemyProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["bank", "run", "set", "light", "head", "line"]
        figures = {"senses_of_bank": 3, "example_words": 6, "division_types": 2, "orders": 3}
        def transform(x):
            return len(x)
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SenseDivisionandPolysemyProgram().run())

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["She sat on a chair", "He ran fast", "It is a good book", "They live here", "We saw a dog", "She likes tea"]
figures = {"example_sentences": 6, "qualities": 3, "sources": 2, "functions": 3}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

class IllustrativeExamplesProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""

Cross-References JAH-LEXGS-000015 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature original treatment; design-study, never presented as established fact; demo machine-checked by execution, facts checked against the public account of the same topic.

Signature treatment: Cross-references are links in a dictionary from one entry to another, marked 'see', 'compare', or 'opposite', guiding readers to related words, to synonyms, or to the main entry for a phrase. They let a dictionary function as a network of words rather than a list, and in digital dictionaries they are hyperlinks. Good cross-referencing is systematic, with every reference pointing to a real entry. In practice this means working through real examples such as see, compare, opposite, also: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part. This is an original Signature treatment of the topic, presented as a design study for teaching and for building Signature language tools, not as a new established fact about the language.

History

Cross-references were used in Johnson's Dictionary and systematised in the Oxford English Dictionary. Digital dictionaries made them hyperlinks, and the study of dictionary structure treats cross-referencing as part of the dictionary's access structure. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

The Problem

What exactly is cross-references, how is it identified in real language data, and how can a reader tell a genuine case from a look-alike? The record answers that question for the topic 'links between dictionary entries': it states the definition, the diagnostic tests, the formal representation, and the limits of the analysis, using the examples see, compare, opposite as its working material.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the definition. Cross-references are links in a dictionary from one entry to another, marked 'see', 'compare', or 'opposite', guiding readers to related words, to synonyms, or to the main entry for a phrase. They let a dictionary function as a network of words rather than a list, and in digital dictionaries they are hyperlinks. Good cross-referencing is systematic, with every reference pointing to a real entry. Step 2: collect the examples see, compare, opposite, also, synonym, antonym and sort them by the diagnostic test for this topic. Step 3: apply the formal rule entry A --see--> entry B to each example in turn, noting which examples change and which stay the same. Step 4: count the results using the key figures {'reference_types': 6, 'example_markers': 6, 'functions': 3, 'media': 2}, checking that the counts add up and that every example is accounted for. Step 5: compare the outcome with the sources cited below and record the limits in the skeptical analysis, so that the answer is checkable rather than asserted.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["see", "compare", "opposite", "also", "synonym", "antonym"]
figures = {"reference_types": 6, "example_markers": 6, "functions": 3, "media": 2}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class CrossReferencesProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["see", "compare", "opposite", "also", "synonym", "antonym"]
        figures = {"reference_types": 6, "example_markers": 6, "functions": 3, "media": 2}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(CrossReferencesProgram().run())

Lemmatisation JAH-LEXGS-000016 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature original treatment; design-study, never presented as established fact; demo machine-checked by execution, facts checked against the public account of the same topic.

Signature treatment: Lemmatisation is the reduction of inflected word forms to their base form, or lemma, so that 'runs', 'ran', and 'running' are all filed under 'run'. It is essential for corpus analysis, for dictionary making, and for search, where a query for 'run' should find all its forms. Lemmatisation requires knowledge of morphology and of irregular forms, and it differs from stemming, which merely cuts off endings. In practice this means working through real examples such as runs, ran, running, run: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part. This is an original Signature treatment of the topic, presented as a design study for teaching and for building Signature language tools, not as a new established fact about the language.

History

Lemmatisation was a practical task in building concordances and dictionaries, and it became a computational task with the growth of corpus linguistics. Modern lemmatisers use morphological rules and dictionaries of irregular forms, and they are a standard component of natural language processing. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

import hashlib
inputs = ["runs", "ran", "running", "run", "dogs", "dog"]
figures = {"forms_of_run": 4, "example_forms": 6, "methods": 2, "irregular_forms": 1}
def transform(x):
    return len(x.rstrip('s'))
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class LemmatisationProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["runs", "ran", "running", "run", "dogs", "dog"]
        figures = {"forms_of_run": 4, "example_forms": 6, "methods": 2, "irregular_forms": 1}
        def transform(x):
            return len(x.rstrip('s'))
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)

            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(LemmatisationProgram().run())

Frequency Information JAH-LEXGS-000017 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature original treatment; design-study, never presented as established fact; demo machine-checked by execution, facts checked against the public account of the same topic.

Signature treatment: Frequency information in a dictionary tells readers how common a word or sense is, often with stars, bands, or numbers from corpus counts. The word 'the' is the most frequent word in English, occurring about 7 percent of all words, while most words are rare. Frequency guides learners to the words worth learning first and guides editors to the senses worth listing first. In practice this means working through real examples such as the, of, and, to: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part. This is an original Signature treatment of the topic, presented as a design study for teaching and for building Signature language tools, not as a new established fact about the language.

History

Frequency information became possible with corpus linguistics, notably the Brown Corpus and the British National Corpus, and it is now a standard feature of learners' dictionaries, which mark the most frequent 3,000 words. The study of word frequency also underlies Zipf's law, which describes the distribution of word frequencies. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["the", "of", "and", "to", "in", "a"]
figures = {"most_frequent_word": "the", "frequency_percent": 7, "example_words": 6, "top_words_marked": 3000}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
