Full working program

import hashlib
class FrequencyInformationProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["the", "of", "and", "to", "in", "a"]
        figures = {"most_frequent_word": "the", "frequency_percent": 7, "example_words": 6, "top_words_marked": 3000}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(FrequencyInformationProgram().run())

Collocations in Dictionaries JAH-LEXGS-000018 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature original treatment; design-study, never presented as established fact; demo machine-checked by execution, facts checked against the public account of the same topic.

Signature treatment: Collocations are words that typically occur together, such as 'strong tea' but not 'powerful tea', or 'make a decision' but not 'do a decision'. Dictionaries for learners now list the most important collocations for each headword, because knowing a word means knowing its partners. Collocations are identified from corpora by statistical measures of association. In practice this means working through real examples such as strong tea, make a decision, heavy rain, fast car: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part. This is an original Signature treatment of the topic, presented as a design study for teaching and for building Signature language tools, not as a new established fact about the language.

History

Collocation was studied by J. R. Firth, who said 'you shall know a word by the company it keeps', and developed in corpus linguistics by Sinclair and others. Collocation dictionaries, notably the Oxford Collocations Dictionary, collect the most useful combinations for learners and writers. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

import hashlib
inputs = ["strong tea", "make a decision", "heavy rain", "fast car", "good luck", "take a break"]
figures = {"collocations_listed": 6, "example_collocations": 6, "measures": 2, "dictionaries": 2}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class CollocationsinDictionariesProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["strong tea", "make a decision", "heavy rain", "fast car", "good luck", "take a break"]
        figures = {"collocations_listed": 6, "example_collocations": 6, "measures": 2, "dictionaries": 2}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(CollocationsinDictionariesProgram().run())

Macrostructure and Microstructure JAH-LEXGS-000019 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature original treatment; design-study, never presented as established fact; demo machine-checked by execution, facts checked against the public account of the same topic.

Signature treatment: Macrostructure is the organisation of a whole dictionary: its word list, its order, and its front and back matter. Microstructure is the organisation of a single entry: its headword, pronunciation, senses, examples, and notes. A dictionary's quality depends on both: a good macrostructure makes words findable, and a good microstructure makes each word understandable. In practice this means working through real examples such as wordlist, order, front matter, headword: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part. This is an original Signature treatment of the topic, presented as a design study for teaching and for building Signature language tools, not as a new established fact about the language.

History

The distinction between macrostructure and microstructure was made in metalexicography, the study of dictionaries, in the twentieth century, notably by Hausmann and Wiegand. It is now the standard framework for describing dictionary design and for comparing dictionaries. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

import hashlib
inputs = ["wordlist", "order", "front matter", "headword", "senses", "examples"]
figures = {"structures": 2, "example_parts": 6, "levels": 2, "frameworks": 1}
def transform(x):
    return len(x.split())
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Specialised Dictionaries JAH-LEXGS-000020 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature original treatment; design-study, never presented as established fact; demo machine-checked by execution, facts checked against the public account of the same topic.

Signature treatment: A specialised dictionary covers one field, such as medicine, law, or engineering, giving the terms of that field with definitions for its practitioners. A medical dictionary defines 'myocardial infarction' for doctors and nurses, while a legal dictionary defines 'tort' for lawyers. Specialised dictionaries are built with experts in the field and with corpora of specialist texts. In practice this means working through real examples such as medical, legal, engineering, chemical: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["medical", "legal", "engineering", "chemical", "financial", "musical"]
figures = {"fields_listed": 6, "example_fields": 6, "users": 3, "corpora": 2}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class SpecialisedDictionariesProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["medical", "legal", "engineering", "chemical", "financial", "musical"]
        figures = {"fields_listed": 6, "example_fields": 6, "users": 3, "corpora": 2}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SpecialisedDictionariesProgram().run())

Reverse Dictionaries JAH-LEXGS-000021 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature original treatment; design-study, never presented as established fact; demo machine-checked by execution, facts checked against the public account of the same topic.

Signature treatment: A reverse dictionary lets a reader find a word from its meaning, as when a reader who wants the word for 'fear of heights' looks it up and finds 'acrophobia'. Reverse dictionaries are organised by concept, by definition, or by a word's letters, and they serve writers, crossword solvers, and people with word-finding difficulties. Digital reverse dictionaries search definitions for the words a reader types. In practice this means working through real examples such as acrophobia, fear of heights, word-finding, concept: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part. This is an original Signature treatment of the topic, presented as a design study for teaching and for building Signature language tools, not as a new established fact about the language.

History

Reverse dictionaries have existed since the eighteenth century, and Roget's Thesaurus serves as one. Modern reverse dictionaries are databases of definitions, and they are used in natural language processing for word-finding and for the tip-of-the-tongue problem. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

inputs = ["acrophobia", "fear of heights", "word-finding", "concept", "definition", "search"]
figures = {"example_words": 6, "organisation_types": 3, "users": 3, "functions": 2}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'

    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"

result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"

print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")

Dictionary Use Research JAH-LEXGS-000022 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature original treatment; design-study, never presented as established fact; demo machine-checked by execution, facts checked against the public account of the same topic.

Signature treatment: Dictionary use research studies how people actually consult dictionaries: what they look up, whether they find it, and whether they understand what they find. Studies show that learners often fail to find the right sense, that they ignore pronunciation and grammar information, and that they prefer bilingual dictionaries. The findings guide dictionary design, teaching, and the development of digital dictionaries. In practice this means working through real examples such as look up, find sense, understand, ignore grammar: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["look up", "find sense", "understand", "ignore grammar", "prefer bilingual", "use app"]
figures = {"studies": 3, "example_behaviours": 6, "methods": 3, "findings": 3}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class DictionaryUseResearchProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["look up", "find sense", "understand", "ignore grammar", "prefer bilingual", "use app"]
        figures = {"studies": 3, "example_behaviours": 6, "methods": 3, "findings": 3}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}

Spelling and Orthography in Dictionaries JAH-LEXGS-000023 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature original treatment; design-study, never presented as established fact; demo machine-checked by execution, facts checked against the public account of the same topic.

Signature treatment: Spelling and orthography in dictionaries record how words are spelled, including variants such as 'colour' and 'color', and the spelling of inflected forms. Dictionaries must choose a standard spelling, note variants, and show where a word's spelling is irregular. Spelling is a matter of convention, recorded by dictionaries rather than decided by them, and it changes slowly over time. In practice this means working through real examples such as colour, color, organise, organize: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part. This is an original Signature treatment of the topic, presented as a design study for teaching and for building Signature language tools, not as a new established fact about the language.

History

Spelling was standardised in part by dictionaries, notably Johnson's Dictionary and Webster's American Dictionary (1828), which fixed many American spellings. Modern dictionaries record both British and American spellings and note the variants in use. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Variant Spellings and Variants JAH-LEXGS-000024 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature original treatment; design-study, never presented as established fact; demo machine-checked by execution, facts checked against the public account of the same topic.

Signature treatment: Variant spellings are the different spellings a word can take, such as 'organise' and 'organize', or 'judgement' and 'judgment'. Dictionaries list variants, mark which is the main form, and cross-refer to it from the others. Variants arise from history, from regional standards, and from reform, and they are a test of a dictionary's coverage and of its editorial policy. In practice this means working through real examples such as organise, organize, judgement, judgment: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part. This is an original Signature treatment of the topic, presented as a design study for teaching and for building Signature language tools, not as a new established fact about the language.

History

Variants were recorded by Johnson and by Webster, whose choices shaped American spelling. Modern dictionaries list variants with their frequency and region, and digital dictionaries accept any variant as a search term. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Signature treatment: Variant spellings are the different spellings a word can take, such as 'organise' and 'organize', or 'judgement' and 'judgment'. History

Variants were recorded by Johnson and by Webster, whose choices shaped American spelling. Modern dictionaries list variants with their frequency and region, and digital dictionaries accept any variant as a search term. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["organise", "organize", "judgement", "judgment", "grey", "gray"]
figures = {"variant_pairs": 3, "example_words": 6, "origins": 3, "standards": 2}
def transform(x):
    return len(x)
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class VariantSpellingsandVariantsProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["organise", "organize", "judgement", "judgment", "grey", "gray"]
        figures = {"variant_pairs": 3, "example_words": 6, "origins": 3, "standards": 2}
        def transform(x):
            return len(x)
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(VariantSpellingsandVariantsProgram().run())
